# THEMIS AUREA,
<note>Themis was the Greek goddess of divine law and order; "Aurea" means golden. Maier presents the rules of the Rosicrucians as a form of divine justice.</note>

->That is/<-

## Of the Laws/
## and Ordinances of the
## Laudable Fraternity
## R. C.
<note>R. C. stands for the Rose Cross (Rosicrucians), a mysterious brotherhood of scholars and healers that claimed to possess secret wisdom.</note>

->A Detailed Treatise<-
->and Report/<-

In which it is thoroughly proven that these same laws are not only consistent with the truth, but are also in themselves necessary, useful, and profitable for both the common and private good.

->Written by<-
->MICHAEL MAIER,<-
->Imperial Counselor, Count Palatine, Knight Exempt, Doctor of Philosophy<-
->and Medicine.<-

# Content and Index
## of all chapters contained herein.

### CHAPTER I.

A demonstration <note>original: "Erweisung." A formal presentation of evidence or logical proof.</note> that all laws and ordinances, over which the goddess *Themis* <note>The Greek goddess of divine law and order, typically represented as the personification of justice.</note> was placed by the Ancients, are intended for the profitable benefit and use of those for whom they were established.

### CHAPTER II.

A demonstration that the laws and ordinances which the first founder of the *Society of the Rose Cross* <note>original: "Societet R. C." referring to the Rosicrucians, a legendary secret society of doctors, alchemists, and philosophers.</note>—who is indeed unknown to others—established, are in themselves good, just, and permissible.

### CHAPTER III.

What the effect of these laws is in general, as well as other circumstances regarding the place, their means, the final purpose <note>original: "endlichen Zwecke." This refers to the "final cause" or ultimate goal in a philosophical sense.</note>, and the time.

## CHAPTER IV.

Concerning the first law of this Order, and the excellence of Medicine, or the Healing Art <note>original: "Arzney Kunst." This term refers to the practical and skilled application of medical knowledge.</note>, compared to other arts; to which this Society has also given precedence over all others.

## CHAPTER V.

That the practice of medicine with its extraordinary remedies—which are linked to hidden properties <term>hidden properties: Also known as "occult qualities," these were natural powers or effects (like magnetism) that were not immediately visible but could be harnessed by a skilled physician or alchemist.</term> and used by the Brothers of this Society—is most highly suited to human nature and is far more effective against diseases than any other methods.

## CHAPTER VI.

That although other physicians receive their fair and honest reward, the Brothers <note>original Latin: "Fratres." The members of the Rosicrucian Order addressed each other as brothers to signify their spiritual and professional fraternity.</note> of this Society heal the sick entirely for free and pay no heed to money or payment.

# Table of Contents.

## CHAPTER IV.

Concerning the first law of this Order, and the excellence of Medicine—or the Art of Healing—compared to other arts, to which this Society <note>The "Society" refers to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood, the subject of this manuscript.</note> has given precedence over all others.

## CHAPTER V.

That the practice of medicine, along with its singular remedies linked to hidden properties <note>original: "verborgenen Eygenſchafften." This refers to "occult" or "sympathetic" qualities in nature that were not visible to the naked eye but were believed to be powerful for healing.</note> which the brothers <note>original: "Fratres"</note> of this Society use, is most highly suited to human nature and is far more effective against diseases than other methods.

## CHAPTER VI.

That although other physicians receive their fair and honest reward, the brothers of this Society nevertheless heal the sick entirely for free, and pay no heed at all to money or salary.

The second abuse: that some physicians 
give their medications magnificent, grand 
titles, even though such diseases

can certainly use both types according to the 
requirements of the different cases.

# Of the Laws and Ordinances of the Laudable Fraternity or Society
### R. C. <note>The initials refer to the "Rose Cross," or Rosicrucians, a mysterious brotherhood of scholars and healers.</note>

# The I. Chapter.

## Demonstration that all laws and ordinances, over which the goddess Themis <note>In Greek mythology, Themis is the personification of divine law, order, and custom.</note> was placed by the ancients, are intended to be directed toward the profitable benefit and use of those for whom they were established.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'O' depicting a seated figure amidst foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

**O**lthough, Kind Reader, a great difference may be perceived among the laws and ordinances of men, which have been established in various ways here and there

# Index

[They] <note>The text likely refers to the members of the Society of the Rose Cross mentioned in the previous chapter summary.</note> intend no Reformation <note>In this context, 'Reformation' refers to a radical or forced restructuring of society and the church.</note> in the world / regarding religion / the conversion of the Jews / or a change of polities <note>Original: 'Policeyen'. This refers to civil administration, public order, and the general structure of government.</note> / as some visionaries <note>Original: 'Phantaſten'. # Of the Laws

...ceived, which takes its origin both from the unique considerations and qualities of the **legislator**, as well as from those for whom such laws are prescribed. Yet, despite these differences, so long as they originate from pious, virtue-loving **rulers** and not from **tyrants**, they are all directed toward a single purpose and end: namely, that they are founded solely upon the general benefit and prosperity of those for whom they were established. 

For indeed, all good laws and ordinances are introduced and established to maintain the community and <term>civil order</term><note>original: "Policey"; in the 17th century, this term referred to the general administration, social regulation, and well-ordering of a community, rather than just a modern police force.</note> among men, as well as to propagate the <term>fear of God</term><note>original: "Gottesforcht"; a standard term for piety and religious devotion.</note>, **justice**, and all virtue, so that no one is burdened beyond what is due. In this purpose, they are primarily directed toward the beneficial use of the common good and the <term>Republic</term><note>original: "Republic"; from the Latin "Res publica," meaning the commonwealth or the state.</note>.

# Concerning the Laws

*Ovid* writes in the first book of the *Metamorphoses* <note>original: "Ovidius 1. Metam."</note> that this goddess was consulted for her counsel on how the human race might be restored and raised up again, having perished in the Great Flood of that time.

Likewise, the following allegorical fable is read concerning her. Because this *Themis* far surpassed other <term>Nymphs<note>Minor nature deities in Greek mythology, often associated with specific locations like forests or water.</note></term> of her time in both high understanding and wisdom, as well as in the beauty of her body, she was greatly loved by the god *Jupiter*. However, as she repeatedly rejected such a marriage and avoided his company and presence with all diligence, she was finally caught in *Macedonia* and forced to marry him. She bore him the *Horae* <note>The "Hours" or "Seasons," who in this context represent the personified components of a stable and flourishing society.</note>—namely, <term>Lawfulness<note>original Greek: εὐνομίαν (Eunomia)</note></term>, <term>Justice<note>original Greek: δίκην (Dike)</note></term>, and <term>Peace<note>original Greek: εἰρήνην (Eirene)</note></term>. Likewise, she also bore <term>Dius Fidius<note>original: "Medium Fidius"; a Roman deity associated with oaths, truth, and the protection of faith between people.</note></term>, who was also called *Sanctus*, or "the Holy One." Because the heathens of that time believed that he was a god of loyalty and faith, the <term>jurament<note>original: "Jurament"; a formal, sacred oath.</note></term> and vow sworn in his name were held to be indissoluble and highly sacred. Through this <term>Eunomia</term>, or good laws, the kingdoms of the earth are protected; all good <term>polities<note>original: "Policeyen"; in the 17th century, this referred to the general administration, order, and welfare of a city or state rather than a modern police force.</note></term>, governments, cities, and lands are preserved; and even small, insignificant things are brought to a state of great and magnificent growth. # On the Laws

The customs of all peoples are conditioned in the same way as the laws are constituted. Indeed, this **Eunomia** <term>Good Order; a Greek term referring to the condition of being well-governed</term> is for humanity, as it were, a certain guiding line or rule of life, according to which all deeds and counsel of men are to be directed. Just as **Polycletus** <note>Polykleitos (5th century BCE), a renowned Greek sculptor famous for his "Canon," a treatise on the ideal mathematical proportions of the human body</note>, an excellent sculptor, wrote a particular book on the proportion of all members of the human body, according to the properties of its parts and the whole body, and called it a Rule <note>original: "Regul" (Latin: Canon)</note>, according to which other craftsmen might also direct their statues.

Similarly, in all arts and sciences there are certain **Axiomata** <term>Axioms; fundamental principles or self-evident truths that serve as the foundation for an argument or science</term>, which, starting from the beginning and foundation of that same art, extend to its final completion. In governments <note>original: "Regimenten," referring to the administration of a state</note>, however, it is this **Eunomia** alone which governs the customs and lives of all people, [preventing them] from deviating into shameful vices and plunging both others and themselves into great misfortune, as is otherwise commonly accustomed to happen. Since, just as many illnesses arise from excessive pleasure and overindulgence, so too does injustice plunge people into great misfortune as their deserved punishment. And on the contrary

<summary>This page compares the concept of "Eunomia" (good legal order) to the artistic "Canon" of the sculptor Polykleitos, arguing that just as art requires proportions, human society requires laws to prevent moral and social collapse.</summary>
<keywords>Eunomia, Polykleitos, proportions, laws, axioms, government, justice, morality, social order</keywords>

It brings many excellent benefits to humanity; and since this is self-evident, there is no need to elaborate on it further.

# Chapter II.

## Demonstration that the laws and ordinances which the first founder of the Society of the Rosy Cross established—though unknown to others—are in themselves good, equitable, and permissible.

### of the Fraternity R. C. <note>original: "der Fraternitet R. C."; refers to the Fraternity of the Rosy Cross</note>

In which we then first wish to take up the <term>Author</term>, the founder and originator of this Order, and consider whether it was proper for him to prescribe such laws and ordinances for himself and others; and also who he may be, and why his name has remained hidden and unknown until now. 

And indeed, it is beyond all doubt that the power to establish laws and ordinances—which bind and obligate all subject peoples—belongs to the supreme head, especially the Roman Emperor <note>referring to the Holy Roman Emperor, who at the time of this writing was the nominal secular leader of much of Central Europe</note>, and thereafter also to every other king who holds such sovereign authority, as well as to certain princes, other lords, and free cities. But the laws ordered by <term>lower authorities</term> <note>original: "unter Oberkeiten"</note>, who are themselves still under the power of a higher sovereignty, do not extend further than to their own subjects; nor are they perpetual, nor may they infringe upon or hinder common Imperial law, but are instead only certain <term>statutes</term> <note>original: "Statuten"</note> directed toward specific places, persons, or matters. Among the ancients, however, the wisest and most discerning men were chosen, so that they, in a certain

# Of the Laws

...establish laws and ordinances, especially when such do not run contrary to reason and <term>fairness</term> <note>original: "Billichkeit"; refers to the concept of equity or natural justice</note>. For among the godly, everything is received and handled for the best, as the poet says; but as for the alliances of the wicked and godless, just as they are unlawful and harmful, so too are the laws and conditions established between them. Their loyalty and faith are nothing but pure deceit; their stability is a disruption; their <term>oaths</term> <note>original: "Jurament"; from the Latin 'iuramentum'</note> and duties are curses; their rules are injustices and abuses; and their laws are shameful incitements.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Regarding the laws and ordinances of this <term>Society</term> <note>original: "Societet"</note>, it is true that the author and founder of the same is a private individual and not a public authority; however, in his administration, he held such great prestige that he composed and bound himself and others with certain rules. He did this both as an orderly <term>head of a household</term> <note>original: "Haußvatter"; a central figure in early modern social structure, responsible for the moral and physical welfare of all under his roof</note> and master of his dwelling, and then also as the first founder and originator of his philosophical <term>Medical Order</term> <note>original: "Arzt-Ordens"; likely referring to the Rosicrucians' primary vow to heal the sick freely</note>. If, however, someone else, who is subject to him by no right or title—

# Concerning the Laws

Since this <term>lawgiver</term><note>original: "Gesetzgeber"</note> / and <term>founder</term><note>original: "Stiffter"</note> of the <term>Fraternity</term><note>original: "Fraternitet"</note> / wished to remain unknown and hidden in name / someone might perhaps harbor all the more doubt because of it / and we therefore wish to provide an answer to this.

It is true / and this founder of the <term>Society</term><note>original: "Societet"</note> remains hidden / because he fell asleep in the Lord many years ago / indeed, he is even hidden from the currently living members of the Fraternity for that reason / nevertheless / they still have his name and lineage in their secret and hidden books / as well as the great and important reasons / why they keep him hidden from the common people. # Chapter IV.

## Concerning the first law of this Order / and concerning the excellence of Medicine or the Art of Healing compared to other arts / to which this Society has also given precedence over others.

We shall now make our beginning with the first law and obligation of this Society / namely: That no one of this Society shall profess any other vocation / but to cure the sick / and that entirely for free. <note>This "first law" is a cornerstone of Rosicrucian ethics: the requirement that a brother's primary public function be the charitable and unpaid healing of the sick.</note>

Where this particular quality of a <term>simple</term> <note>original: *simplicis*; in early modern medicine, a "simple" was a medicinal herb or substance used on its own, rather than as part of a complex compound.</note> is intended to **operate** and **work**, one must not overwhelm or mix it with many others which are (as is supposed) warm, cold, moist, or dry in this or 그 *degree* <note>Humoral theory categorized all substances by four primary qualities (heat, cold, moisture, dryness) and four degrees of intensity.</note> and—on account of the first quality—intended to **alter** <note>original: *alteriren*; to change the humoral balance of the body</note> or to make [the humors] thick or thin, and so forth. At this point, however, someone might ask what <term>specific quality</term> <note>original: *qualitas specifica*</note> is called in these parts. Answer: It is precisely that which the **physicians** <note>original: *Medici*</note> otherwise call a hidden property <note>Often referred to as "occult" properties, these were healing powers that could not be explained by the simple balance of hot/cold/wet/dry qualities.</note>, because it presumably cannot be derived from the warm, cold, moist, and dry primary qualities.

# Of the Laws

That which of itself is the acting part of the substance, and is not of the quality, acts from the whole substance.

And it is to be noted here that the opinion of **Averroës** <note>Ibn Rushd (1126–1198), an influential Andalusian philosopher and physician.</note>, cited above, was that the actual **complexion** <note>The specific balance of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—within a body.</note>—according to the manner in which it inclines toward the **quality** and characteristic of the **object** <note>original: *obiecti Qualitet*</note> with a certain **proportion**—is called the whole **property** <note>original: *Proprietet*</note> and characteristic.

However, so that we may explain ourselves better and more clearly, we say that the whole substance, or characteristic, through which the **compound** <note>original: *mixtum*; a physical body formed by the mixing of elements.</note> produces a particular **effect**, the cause of which—speaking naturally—is hidden, is a power or natural characteristic that immediately encompasses the <term>substantial form</term> <note>original: *Substantial Form*; the essential nature or "soul" of a substance that gives it its identity and powers beyond its material parts.</note>. This arises through the **influence** of the heavenly bodies, which have a **special influence** in this regard, by the receiving of a **form**. It also appears that **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician and philosopher whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard text in Europe for centuries.</note> in [the Canon, Book 2, Fen 1, Doctrine 1, Summary 1, Chapter 15] and in the [book *On the Powers of the Heart*, Tractate 1, final chapter] <note>original: *lib. de virib. cord. tract. 1. c. vlt.*</note> is of this opinion.

64 Of the Laws

This **property** <note>original: *Proprietet*; refers to a specific occult or hidden power inherent in a substance.</note> can be described in this manner: That it is a power, or natural characteristic, which immediately and inseparably comprises the **substantial form** <note>original: *Substantial Form*; the essential nature or "soul" of a substance that gives it its identity and powers beyond its material parts.</note> of a thing—which is enclosed in a certain proportion—with the help of a celestial **influence** <note>original: *Influenz*; in this context, the flow of power from the stars or heavens into earthly matter.</note>.

From what was previously stated, it follows that the whole property is not a **complexion** <note>original: *Complexion*; the specific balance of the four humors (hot, cold, moist, dry) within a body or substance.</note>; for if it were so, those items which possess a similar characteristic would also have an identical or equal **complexion**. But the **logical consequence** <note>original: *Consequens*</note> is false; for **rhubarb** <note>original: *Rhabarbarum*</note> and **tamarinds** <note>original: *Tamarinden*</note> attract **choler** <note>original: *Choleram*; yellow bile, one of the four humors.</note> according to their whole characteristic, yet they are not of the same **complexion**. Thus far speaks **Valescus** <note>Valescus de Taranta (c. 1382–1418), a famous Portuguese physician whose works were standard medical textbooks.</note>.

From this it now appears that the characteristics of **medicines** <note>original: *Medicamenten*</note> are recognized more through experience than through a certain rule of reason, by which even the nature of the lowliest **simple** <note>original: *Simplicis*; a "simple" is a single medicinal herb or mineral used as an ingredient, rather than a compound mixture.</note> cannot be fully fathomed. ## Chapter VI.

### That although other doctors receive their fair and honest reward, the Brothers of this Society nevertheless heal the sick entirely for free, and pay no heed at all to gold or wages.

We read in various histories of many excellent physicians <note>original: Medicis</note>, who were not only held in high esteem by great kings, princes, and potentates, but were also—on account of curing a specific illness or by way of a yearly salary <note>original: Bestallung</note>—gifted with an exceedingly large sum of money or raised to high honors. Among these, <term>confusion<note>original: "Confusion"; the author uses both the Latin-derived "Confusion" and the German "Verwirrung" to emphasize total disorder.</note></term> and disorder are the primary causes; for in such a massive army, there must necessarily be many different nations, peoples, languages, religions, and commanders. From all of this, great confusion arises, eventually leading to ultimate downfall and ruin.

The same holds true for <term>medicinal remedies<note>original: "Artney Remedien"</note></term>: when they are too many in number, they conflict with one another more than they fight the disease. This is due to their various mixed and distinct properties, where each one hinders the other. Yet, only that property which is <term>akin to nature<note>This reflects the historical medical belief that treatment must harmonize with the body's natural "vital force" to be effective.</note></term> is powerful enough to resist the disease and drive it out.

# Of the Laws

To worship the sun <note>To "worship the sun" was a common early modern idiom for sycophancy—flattering those currently in power or "on the rise."</note>—that is, to align oneself with other high-ranking persons out of greed and ambition—is a common practice. It is written of the statue of Diana that it was so rigged through the trickery of the priests that it appeared quite cheerful to those who offered great gifts; however, it looked upon others who brought nothing with a serious, threatening face. This is also how things are still ordered in the world today, and it follows the old little verse:

->Homer, with all his great art,<-
->Without money, loses all favor.<-

Indeed, it is entirely a "golden age" <note>The author uses "golden age" ironically here to describe an era obsessed with gold/money rather than a literal age of peace.</note>, and everything is for sale for money; everything is ruled, maintained, confirmed, concealed, brought to ruin, and overturned by money.

But those who completely disregard—or consider as the least significant, almost as a <term>parergon</term> <note>original: "Parergon" — a Greek term for a secondary task or a "side-project" that is incidental to one's main work</note>—that which others consider the highest and best treasure (though other excellent <term>ingenia</term> <note>original: "Ingenia" — a Latin term for natural talents, characters, or brilliant minds</note> are deeply troubled by the lack of it), possess little or nothing of this earthly soil or money. And the little saying of Phocylides <note>An ancient Greek gnomic poet known for his moral maxims</note> is the ultimate truth: "Gold and silver are always but a deception to men." O gold, how you are a forerunner <note>The text likely refers to gold as a "leader" or "precursor" to all manner of sins or social ills</note>...

It is a great vice / (says Damascenus) <note>Likely referring to Johannes Damascenus, an influential figure whose name was often associated with early medical aphorisms.</note> to make use of composites <term>Compositen</term> / and medications <term>Medicamenten</term> <note>original: "Medicamenten"</note> put together from many pieces / when the simples <term>Simplicien</term> <note>A "simple" is a medicinal herb or plant used on its own, rather than as part of a complex mixture.</note> are sufficient for such a purpose. Why then do these men not desist from such a vice / and follow this rule? For they themselves know / that a great quantity easily causes confusion / especially among the simples of nature / whose properties are not at all known / or understood.

We see an example of this at an Imperial Diet <term>Reichstag</term> <note>The supreme deliberative assembly of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> or a Provincial Diet <term>Prouincialtag</term> of a great land / when many people come together in such a place / and each one in particular gives his own voice / the matter is drawn into many different opinions / as many heads as there are <note>A variation of the Latin proverb *quot homines, tot sententiae*: "as many men, so many opinions."</note> / so too is no unity to be hoped for / unless it is handed over to a few of them alone / who are then commonly great princes and lords / who are drawn into such deliberations <term>Rhatschlägen</term>.

## Of the Laws

A similar confusion and impurity is also perceived in many simples <term>Simplicien</term> <note>In early medicine, a "simple" was a medicinal herb or plant used on its own, as opposed to a "compound" or "composite" mixture of many ingredients.</note>, for each one possesses its own unique active qualities. When these are mixed and drawn together, they are split into as many different directions as there are ingredients in the number.

It is therefore highly necessary to select a few from a great multitude, and the best from the good, which are meant to resist the sickness and support nature. When this is done, the physician <term>Medicus</term> will be esteemed as highly learned—not because of the great quantity of ingredients, but because of their specific properties. The apothecary <term>Apotecker</term> will also have sufficient profit, and the patient will expel the illness with little expense <term>Vncosten</term> while strengthening nature in its operation.

For not everything that exceeds another in size is necessarily better, as can be seen in both mankind and gold. The irrational animals <term>vnvernünfftige Thier</term>—oxen, horses, deer—have large heads, but man has a great head <note>The original German uses *Haupt* here for the human head, a term often carrying more dignity or spiritual significance than the word *Kopf* used for animals.</note> with far more brain and wisdom than such beasts. # Chapter 8

## The second abuse: that some *physicians* <note>original: *Medici*</note> give their *medicines* <note>original: *Medicamentẽ*</note> glorious and magnificent *titles* <note>original: *Titul*</note>, even though such diseases could be *cured* <note>original: *curiret*</note> with modest and well-known remedies (though not for nothing and without reward).

Along with the previous abuses, there comes this one as well, which is harmful to the *patient* but brings equal profit to the *physician* <note>original: *Medico*</note>, albeit under a different name and *title*. Where the former [abuses] sought to sweep out another’s purse <note>original: *den Seckel zufegen*; a 17th-century idiom for overcharging or emptying someone's wallet.</note> through the sheer quantity and number of *medicines*, these latter ones attempt it through their *quality* and preciousness. This is because they notice how everything that is merely rare and expensive is held in much higher regard by others than other common and well-known *prescriptions* <note>original: *Recepten*</note>—and this is not because of their actual goodness and inherent property <note>original: *Eygenschafft*</note>, but rather according to human habit and whim.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page identifies a specific medical "abuse" where physicians use fancy titles and expensive ingredients to overcharge patients, despite the availability of simple, effective cures. It critiques the human tendency to value rare, costly items over common but reliable remedies.</summary>
<keywords>medical ethics, physician greed, expensive remedies vs. simple cures, medical titles, patient exploitation, pharmaceutical history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of medical abuses, specifically how physicians exploit the psychology of the patient. The previous page noted that doctors often use expensive-sounding titles for simple remedies to justify higher fees, taking advantage of the fact that people value the rare over the common.</meta>

<header>of the Fraternity R. C. <note>original: *Fraternitet R. C.*, referring to the Rosicrucian Order</note></header>

Therefore, Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon, a prominent Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> also kept his "golden plaster" hidden for good reason, through which he earned 100 crowns—and would have received much more had he revealed it, even though it was one of the least significant things. For many excellent things are held in low esteem simply because of their abundance and the fact that they are well-known; either because the common man soils everything, or because the judgment and opinion of others considers that which was previously held as precious to be lesser now, and thus diminishes its effect. For while the judgment of another may have no power over me, yet my own **judgment** <note>original: **Iudicium**</note> can cause great movements within me, which either promote the **remedies** <note>original: **Remedien**</note> or hinder their proper effects.

We have examples of this in almost all illnesses, but especially in **melancholy** <note>original: **Melancholia**</note> and **hypochondria** <note>original: **Hypochondriaca**</note>, otherwise called the **cross of physicians** <note>original: **crux Medicorum**; a term for a problem or disease so difficult it torments the doctor to treat it</note>, which is very rarely cured even by highly learned and diligent **physicians** <note>original: **Medicis**</note>, especially when the **patient** <note>original: **Patient**</note> himself [hinders] the cure through false imaginings, as it were...

## Chapter VIII

**The second abuse: that some physicians <note>original: *Medici*</note> give their medications <note>original: *Medicamenten*</note> grand, magnificent titles, even though such illnesses could be cured with modest and well-known remedies (yet not for free, and not without reward).**

To the previous abuses is added this one, which is harmful to the patient but brings the same profit to the physician, though under a different name and title. Whereas the former [abuses] attempt to saw through another’s purse <note>original: *den Seckel zusegen*; a colorful idiom for draining someone's money or "cutting their pockets"</note> through the sheer quantity and number of medications, these [physicians] do so through their [medications'] quality and costliness. This happens because they notice how anything that is rare and expensive is held in much higher regard by others than common and well-known prescriptions <note>original: *Recepten*</note>; and this is not because of their inherent goodness or properties, but rather according to human habit and opinion.

For this reason, Galen also kept his "golden plaster" hidden with good reason, through which he earned 100 crowns <note>A significant sum of gold coins in the early modern period.</note> and would have received much more had he revealed its composition, though it was actually one of the most insignificant of items. For many magnificent things are held in low esteem simply because of their abundance and the fact that they are well-known—either because the common man soils everything <note>The author suggests that when a remedy becomes common knowledge, the public's lack of respect "defiles" its reputation.</note>, or because the discretion and <term>opinion</term> <note>original: *Opinion*</note> of others now holds in lower regard that which was previously considered precious, and thus withdraws its effectiveness. For even if the discretion of another can do nothing against me, nevertheless my own <term>judgment</term> <note>original: *Iudicium*; referring here to one's internal conviction or mental state</note> can cause great movements within me, which either promote the remedies or prevent their proper effects.

## Chapter IX.

### That many among <term>physicians<note>original: "Medicis"</note></term> have an aversion to <term>chemical medicines<note>In this period, "chemical" medicines referred to alchemical or mineral-based remedies, often contrasted with traditional herbal ones.</note></term>, and no less to those made from <term>plants<note>original: "Vegetalien"</note></term> and <term>Galenic remedies<note>Medicines based on the tradition of the ancient Greek physician Galen, which primarily used plant-based mixtures to balance the body's humors.</note></term>; however, neither of these aversions is justified, and one should use both types as the requirements of different cases demand.

# On the Laws #

would the *physicians*<note>original: "Medici"; refers to the university-trained doctors of the period.</note> not diligently strive to inquire into such things and to preserve the *simples*<note>original: "Simplicien"; individual medicinal herbs, minerals, or substances used as the building blocks of medicine.</note>? But it appears as if *Fernelius*<note>Jean Fernel (1497–1558) was a famous French physician who explored "hidden causes" of disease that went beyond traditional Greek theories.</note> in his book *On the Hidden Causes of Things*<note>original: "de abditter. caus." (De abditis rerum causis)</note> concluded that this peculiar and *specific quality*<note>original: "specifica Qualitas"</note>, or property, which he calls the *form*<note>original: "formam"; in philosophy, the "form" is the essential nature or "soul" of an object that gives it its specific properties.</note>, is hidden in all parts of every *simple* and distributed through all the *elements*<note>The four elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>. Therefore, when such a *simple* is distilled through the *chemical art*<note>original: "Chymimische Kunst"; referring to the early scientific and alchemical processes of breaking down substances into their core parts.</note> into water, then into oils, and finally reduced to ash and salt, all such different parts—water, oils, and salt—contain these hidden properties within them. Indeed, each part contains it whole and undivided, yet one part is more powerful than the others; however, when they are put back together, they are then at their most powerful.

From this it follows that the *tangible body*<note>original: "corpus palpabile"</note> of every *simple*, which is crushed, cut, purified, boiled, and mixed with others in its *physical mass*<note>original: "Corputenz"; the bulk or material substance of the ingredient.</note>, is as it were a prison, or a vessel and house for such a peculiar property, by which the disease is cured/

# Concerning the Laws

...is maintained. Rhubarb <note>original: "Rhabarbarum," used here as a medicinal root</note> dries out if it is not protected <note>original: "foviret," from the Latin fovere, meaning to keep warm or cherish; here meaning to preserve or coat</note> with wax. The spirit of wine <note>original: "Spiritus vini," an early term for ethanol or high-proof alcohol</note> and the salt of deer's blood <note>original: "sal sanguinis cervini," likely a chemical salt like hartshorn salt</note> evaporate completely if they are not kept in well-sealed glasses. Yet all these are physical substances <note>original: "corpora," meaning bodies or matter</note>, even if the former are somewhat thicker than the latter.

What, however, should we expect from those unique qualities once they are separated from their bodies and containers? Does not each part return to its corresponding elemental place and region—fire to fire, and air to air? This is certainly believable and in accordance with reason. Furthermore, who can separate the unique and specific quality <note>original: "specificam qualitatem"</note> from fire, which is burning; from water, which is wetting; or from earth, which is drying? If this separation is impossible in simple substances <note>original: "Simplicien," the basic, unmixed ingredients used in medicine</note>, it is even more so in composite or mixed bodies. No one, however, is bound to perform <note>original: "præstiren"</note> impossible things; or if he has obligated himself to do so, he must seek recourse through the restoration to the original state <note>original: "Restitution in integrum," a legal principle allowing a person to be released from a contract and returned to their original position</note>.

# of the Fraternity of the Rosy Cross

From this it follows that the dogmatic physicians <note>original: "Medici dogmatici"; these were university-trained doctors who followed traditional Galenic theory, often criticized by the Rosicrucians and Paracelsians for being too theoretical</note> make use of medicines <note>original: "Medicamenten"</note> that are in accordance with nature and reason, and which are permitted and possible. They refrain from others that are more desired than hoped for, and which are prepared more for outward appearance than for their true inherent quality <note>original: "Eygenschafft"</note>.

For this reason, syrups <note>original: "Syrupen"</note>, juleps <note>original: "Julep," a sweet medicinal drink, often flavored with rosewater</note>, and conserves <note>original: "Conseruen"</note> are not to be despised, provided that the inherent quality hidden within the body of the simple medicine <note>original: "Simplicis," a single, unmixed botanical or mineral ingredient</note> is not overwhelmed and set aside by the excessive amount of sugar. The same applies to electuaries <note>original: "Electuaria," medicinal pastes or powders mixed with honey or syrup</note>, opiates <note>original: "Opiaten"</note>, and antidotes <note>original: "Antidota"</note>, provided that a disorderly confusion <note>original: "vnordentliche Confusion"</note> does not hinder and completely destroy such quality (which cannot be the same in many different ingredients) instead of a proper and fitting mixture. Likewise, pills <note>original: "Pilulen"</note> and all other bitter, sharp, and strong-smelling remedies <note>original: "Remedien"</note> administered in large quantities are acceptable, provided they do not cause such nausea in the stomach of both the healthy and the sick that they would much rather endure the illness than such medicines. If, then, the sharpness, bitterness, and strong smell are the special [qualities]...

### of the Rosicrucian Fraternity

[The practice is] brought back again into its former state / from which it had easily been driven by reckless levity and haste: Just as the world-wise philosophers also diligently command that beautiful little proverb, "Hasten slowly" <note>original: "Eyle mit weile"; the Latin phrase *Festina lente*, a favorite motto of Augustus Caesar, suggesting that activities should be performed with a balance of urgency and diligence</note> / and advise that once plans are decided upon, they should be put into action. Nevertheless, the **dogmatic method** <note>original: "methodus dogmatica"; a school of medical thought emphasizing the search for the hidden causes of disease rather than just treating symptoms</note> of **curing** remains unmoved / and its **axioms** <note>fundamental principles or established truths</note> stay constant: namely, that when the cause is cleared away / the **effects** and impacts are also abolished; when the sickness is **cured** / the **symptoms** also cease; and as **Ovid** <note>Publius Ovidius Naso (43 BC – 17 AD), the Roman poet; the reference is to *Metamorphoses*, where he writes that an incurable wound must be cut away to save the rest of the body</note> says / an incurable wound should be **cured** by cutting / so that the healthy part is not also corrupted.

For even those with uncomprehending **donkeys' ears** <note>a reference to King Midas, who was given donkey ears for his poor judgment; here it implies those too ignorant or stubborn to appreciate alchemical medicine</note> cannot grasp such things / while others will easily understand and be satisfied with it. But we wish now to [turn] to the other

The disciple of *Daedalus*—who invented the saw and the potter’s wheel, as *Ovid* writes—was killed through the hatred of his teacher. <note>original: *Dædali Discipul*. # On the Laws

his victory trophies: *Caesar* against *Cato*, *Hadrian* against *Trajan*, *Marcus Crassus* against *Pompey*, *Alexander* against *Achilles* when he had seen his grave, *Julius Caesar* against *Alexander* at the Temple of *Hercules* on the island of *Gades* <note>original: "Gades"; modern Cádiz in Spain. According to ancient biographers, Caesar wept at the sight of Alexander's statue here because he had achieved so little by the age Alexander had conquered the world.</note>. And who could recount all those who have allowed themselves to be consumed by this vice? It is likewise impossible to fathom the great misfortune and harm that have arisen from it. Among *physicians*, however, this is so much more harmful and reprehensible, as the *subject* and the work with which they deal—namely, the human being—is to be esteemed far above all other things. <note>The author argues that while envy is bad for generals, it is catastrophic for doctors because their "subject" is human life itself.</note> Such envy also frequently breaks out into great quarreling, strife, and *calumnies* <note>original: "Calumnien"; slander or malicious misrepresentation.</note>, which then far exceed even a deep-seated secret envy. Indeed, it is otherwise practically innate to these current times of ours—and common to the highest degree—for everyone to slander and *calumniate*. From this also arise so many shameful and libelous writings, or pasquils <note>original: "Pasquillen"; these were anonymous, often satirical, lampoons or pamphlets used to publicly shame an opponent.</note>, which are to be seen. # Concerning the Laws

<note>original: "seiner Siegszeichen"; referring to trophies or signs of victory.</note> of his trophies; Caesar against Cato, Hadrian against Trajan, Marcus Crassus against Pompey, Alexander against Achilles when he saw his grave, Julius Caesar against Alexander at the Temple of Hercules on the Island of Gades <note>Gades is the ancient Roman name for the modern city of Cádiz, Spain. And who could recount all those who have allowed themselves to be consumed by this vice? Just as it is impossible to fathom what great misfortune and harm has arisen from it. Among physicians, however, this is so much more harmful and reprehensible, the higher and more precious the *subject* <note>original: "Subiectum"; here referring to the human body or the patient as the focus of the doctor's work.</note> and work with which they deal—namely, the human being—is to be esteemed above all other things. This also frequently breaks out into great quarreling, strife, and calumnies <note>original: "Calumnien"; false and malicious statements intended to damage someone's reputation.</note>, which then far exceed the aforementioned secret envy. Indeed, it is otherwise almost innate to these times of ours, and extremely common, to slander and calumniate everyone. From this also so many shameful and defamatory writings, or pasquils <note>original: "Pasquillen"; short satirical protest pieces or lampoons, often posted in public places.</note>, are to be seen. Among others, however, Apelles <note>Apelles of Kos was a renowned painter of ancient Greece. His lost painting "Calumny" was famously described by the writer Lucian and later recreated by Renaissance artists like Botticelli.</note> has properly painted *Calumny*, and Lipsius <note>Justus Lipsius (1547–1606) was a prominent Flemish humanist and philosopher who wrote extensively on politics and ethics.</note> has also aptly described the same, so that it is unnecessary to add anything further to it.

# Of the Laws

[be]ing those who are pledged <term>subarrhirt</term> <note>original: "subarrhirt," from the Latin *subarrhare*, meaning to give an earnest-money pledge or to betroth. The author means they are legally and spiritually bound to God alone.</note> to no one else but God the Highest alone, to whom they also, alongside the highest <term>sovereignty</term> <note>original: "Oberkeit"; referring to the established civil authorities or government.</note>, undoubtedly render obedience. As much as we can perceive from their writings and actions, we see that these brothers publicly confess that they have no foreknowledge at all of their own death and the end of their lives. Since this is true, it follows that with their <term>remedies</term> <note>original: "Remedien."</note>—even though they are very powerful and excellent—they still cannot cure those diseases, whether in themselves or others, which are destined for death.

### of the Fraternity R. C.

...are fatal <note>This completes the word "ar[ten]" or "ar[ising]" from the previous page, referring to diseases of a certain nature or those ordained by fate.</note> or rid themselves of the same. For to undertake such a thing would be to want to resist God’s providence and will.

However, the aforementioned diseases are of three kinds and of three degrees of progression: first, in the beginning; next, when they have somewhat hardened and grown stronger; and then finally, when they have become completely rigid and long-lasting <note>original: "langwirig," meaning chronic.</note>. When they are in the beginning, the majority can also be healed by other **dogmatic physicians** <note>original: "dogmaticis Medicis," referring to university-trained doctors who followed the established medical doctrines of the era, such as those of Galen.</note>; but when they are on the rise and already strong, the Brothers undoubtedly still have **remedies** <note>original: "Remedien."</note> through which they—with the help of the Almighty—may drive such things away. But when the diseases are fatal, they can be helped neither in the beginning nor in the middle; instead, it is soon revealed what kind of end they will reach. But as for the third kind, these are not to be healed at all; to this category belonged the cancer of the breast of the woman at **Wetzlar** <note>A town in Hesse, Germany; the author cites this as a well-known contemporary medical case that resisted treatment.</note>, which could not be healed.

->### of the Fraternity R. C.<-

or also exempt themselves from it. For to undertake such a thing would be to want to resist God's providence and will. There are, however, the aforementioned diseases of three kinds, and of three stages of progression: first in the beginning; thereafter when they have somewhat hardened and become stronger; and then finally, when they have completely stiffened and become chronic.

When they are now in the beginning, the majority can also be healed by other <term>dogmatic physicians</term> <note>original: "dogmaticis Medicis." These were traditional university-trained doctors who relied on established medical theories (dogmas), such as the Galenic system of humors, rather than the "spagyric" or chemical medicine associated with the Rosicrucians.</note>; but when they are in their increase and already strong, then the <term>Brothers</term> <note>original: "Fratres."</note> undoubtedly still have <term>remedies</term> <note>original: "Remedien."</note> through which they—with the help of the Almighty—may drive these away. But when the diseases are fatal, they can be helped neither in the beginning nor in the middle; rather, it soon manifests itself what kind of end they will reach.

As for the third kind, these are not to be healed at all; to which category also belonged the <term>cancer</term> <note>original: "Krebs."</note> on the breast of the woman of <term>Wetzlar</term> <note>A town in Germany. This likely refers to a specific case cited in Rosicrucian or medical literature of the time to demonstrate that certain conditions are divinely ordained and thus beyond human intervention.</note>, which could not be healed.

As for what now concerns the other diseases, just as these [appear] to an experienced

# Of the Laws

## Chapter XIII.

### The second Law of the Fraternity: that in their travels they are not bound to wear any special garment for the sake of the Order, but may adapt themselves according to the customs of each place.

Perhaps, favorable reader, there will be many who, once they have read the preceding explanation of these laws of the Order of the Rosy Cross, will prematurely accuse me—as there is already a great multitude of slanderers in these times—of having treated these matters far too extensively and excessively, and of wishing to make a grand display with my long-winded discourses. # Of the Laws

They change their clothing so that others do not have to change their dispositions <note>original: "Gemüther"; referring to people's inner attitudes or mental states toward them</note>, and for that reason they also seek no special fame, as they wish to remain hidden and unknown. Likewise, they do not accept what is offered to them for the sake of the Fraternity <note>original: "Fraternitet"; the Rosicrucian brotherhood</note>; on the contrary, they accept what is otherwise given by sincere persons. From this, no suspicion of any fraud can be found.

Since it is not forbidden—not only to change one's clothing in different places, when such a thing happens without harm to another and is directed toward a good end. Just as the sign <note>In traditional logic, the "sign" is the outer symbol, while the "good" is the inner truth or essence.</note> does not change the good that is signified, nor does the accidental <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are external qualities—like clothing—that do not change the essential "substance" or nature of a person.</note> abolish the substance. Names and clothing are, as it were, identifying marks and a cloak for individual persons, without which they might be recognized. The ancient **philosophers**, as well as the priests in **Egypt**, used a white linen garment by which they were recognized; just as **Pythagoras**, along with his followers, and **Apollonius** <note>Apollonius of Tyana, a famous Greek teacher and philosopher often compared to Jesus or Pythagoras in early modern literature.</note> did, both for the sake of cleanliness and to indicate their sincerity of mind through such a pure white color.

<summary>This page explains the Rosicrucian law of wearing local clothing to remain anonymous and avoid unearned fame, justifying this through the philosophical distinction between outward appearance and inner substance.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucian laws, anonymity, philosophy of dress, Pythagoras, Apollonius of Tyana, substance vs. accident, Egyptian priests</keywords>

->of the Fraternity of the R.C.<-

They have also never changed this attire <note>original: "Habit"; refers to the distinctive clothing of a religious or professional order</note> without cause, just as monks are also accustomed to do. There are indeed several types of birds to whom Nature has granted this privilege <note>original: "Priuilegiū"</note>: that in winter they change their voice along with their feathers, as can be seen with the cuckoo, so that they are not recognized or persecuted by others.

This is also to be seen in many other animals; in **Livonia**, **Lithuania**, and other northern <note>original: "Mitternächtischen"; literally "midnight-land," a common early modern term for the North</note> places, hares are white in the wintertime, but in summer they have their usual color. The chameleon <note>original: "Chamæleon"</note> changes its color according to the thing that is nearest to it and which it touches; because of this, it also frequently escapes the hunters' snares. The same is also to be seen in certain fish. Ants <note>original: "Omeyssen"</note> and the majority of such insects <note>original: "Vngezieffers"; used here for small crawling creatures in general</note> grow wings and fly through the air, and it is not recognized what they were before.

Now, what nature, reason, law, and custom allow to all people, and what necessity requires—namely, that this **Society of the R.C.** is not bound to a specific dress—why should they not also [with...] <note>The text ends mid-sentence to match the catchword "mit" (with) for the following page.</note>

# Chapter XIV.
## The third law / that they shall come
## together annually on a certain day /
## at their appointed place / so that
## they may both unite with one

For these reasons, these brothers decided that all those associated with the <term>Society<note>original: "Societet," referring here to the Rosicrucian brotherhood.</note></term> should meet annually at a certain place, so that they might not be scattered here and there at all times, having no knowledge of their shared purpose.

# Of the Laws

Just as this law is the third in the sequence, it is also to be preferred above those following it because of its dignity, as by it a true **Philosophical Pythagorean** assembly is annually established. And upon this, such philosophers gather who yield nothing to the ancients in understanding, science, and experience of natural and wondrous things, virtue, and good morals. Nor do they spend the day with useless, foolish questions, but rather discuss the hidden secrets of nature, magnificent mathematical <term>automata<note>original: "automata"; these are mechanical devices designed to move automatically, often using weights or internal gears, highly prized in the early modern period as demonstrations of scientific skill.</note></term>, or works that move themselves by weights, astronomical and astrological inventions, and scholarly disputes. **Pythagoras** <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 570–495 BC) whose followers were known for communal living and rigorous self-examination.</note> admonished his <term>disciples<note>original: "Discipul"</note></term> that they should daily inquire of themselves and consider this little verse: "What have I done? What have I done or accomplished at the right or wrong time?" # Chapter XV.

## The Fourth Law: That every member of this Order is obligated to look for a suitable person to take their place, who may succeed <note>The original German uses the Latin-derived "succediren," meaning to follow after or take over the position of a predecessor.</note> them should the need arise.

Since everything under heaven is transitory and changeable—as the poet <note>This likely refers to classical poets such as Ovid, who famously wrote about the ever-changing nature of the world.</note> also testifies—and especially since in man there is nothing permanent, but rather everything passes away, changes, and is transformed; for this reason, birth and the procreation of children were granted to them by God as a special <term>Remedy <gloss>original: "Remedium"</gloss></term> and means. This, then, is the only means through which the world—which would otherwise easily perish—is preserved, and every [living] thing toward the pro-

Among the Egyptians, children were accustomed—just as they accepted their parents' inheritance—to also practice the same trades as their parents and to learn them by means of a special law. The kings, however, were chosen from among the priests, and the priests from among the philosophers; and the philosophers were also the children of philosophers, though in the arts, father and son are unequal in this regard. And while one can indeed leave temporal goods to one's children, one cannot always pass on the gifts of the mind, especially those which specifically belong to this <term>Fraternity <gloss>original: "Fraternitet"</gloss></term>. To the second question—why they do not bring their secrets publicly to light—it is answered that such an action would be entirely thoughtless and foolish.

For there are many secrets in <term>civil government<note>original: Regiment. Referring to the "arcana imperii" or secrets of statecraft used to maintain social order.</note></term>, and far more in divine matters, and no small number in nature as well, all of which are not to be broadcast among the common people; for this reason, they should not be printed, so that no confusion and disruption arise in the world.

Indeed, an excellent author (<term>Arnoldus Clapmarius<note>Arnoldus Clapmarius (1574–1604) was a German jurist and scholar best known for his work on the "Arcana Rerum Publicarum," which dealt with the secret strategies of politics and power.</note></term>) has already written generally on the secrets of civil government before now; but regarding divine secrets, no one should presume to do the same, even though the works of princes should be made manifest, while the works of God are to be kept secret.

The Knightly Orders have their signs, namely the <term>Knights of Malta</term> <note>A Catholic lay religious order of knights originally founded to provide care for poor, sick, or injured pilgrims to the Holy Land.</note> the double cross, the Order of the <term>Golden Fleece</term> <note>original: *gülden Fluß*; a prestigious order of chivalry. But these Brothers, who no less fight a good knighthood for God, have the R.C. as a special seal, which they indeed do not draw in precious gold, but upon paper. 

The Maltese in former times chose the double cross with great zeal in memory of the Lord Christ’s cross, and thus the sign in place of the thing signified. # of the Fraternity R. C.

...the claws and footprints of the reddish lion <note>A common alchemical symbol often representing the fixed sulfur or the final stage of the "Great Work."</note>, or the little drop of water from the Pegasian spring? <note>Referring to Hippocrene, the fountain on Mount Helicon struck open by the hooves of the winged horse Pegasus; it symbolizes poetic and spiritual inspiration.</note> Although I have hereby revealed neither the services of **Isis** <note>The Egyptian goddess whose mysteries were a primary influence on Western esoteric traditions.</note> nor the **Eleusinian** <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries were the most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</note> rites. And there is no one who understands me, except he who is well-practiced and experienced in both words and deeds; to whom I have said a little, but not everything—I have indicated the *kind* <note>original: *genus*</note>, not the *specific type* <note>original: *speciem*</note>.

There are some who are accustomed to creating *anagrams* <note>original: *Anagrammata*</note> from the letters of a name as a special *sign* <note>original: *omen*</note>; but to consider what sign and fortunate indication is hidden in **R. C.** would be a work well worthy of the labor. The Rose Cross truly signifies something that becomes "ever dearer" <note>The German phrase *je länger je lieber* is also a common name for the honeysuckle plant, suggesting the name R.C. grows in beauty over time.</note>: joy and lamentation, laughter and sadness. For to live among roses and to be under the cross is a contrary image, yet one not unfitting for human life. When a person is born into this world, they begin to cry because of the pain (resulting from the change of place and the umbilical cords through which they were nourished in the mother’s womb), and they do not laugh—as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author, noted in his *Natural History* that human infants are the only creatures not to laugh immediately, often waiting weeks.</note> says—before the 60th day of their life.

Otherwise, however, I wish to regard the character **R.** more as the *substantial* <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "substantial" refers to the essence or core nature of a thing.</note> and the **C.** as the *accidental* <note>The "accidental" refers to the secondary qualities or attributes that are not essential to the thing's core nature.</note> and secondary sign, contrary to the common interpretation of the **Rose Cross**.

# Of the Laws

...or to expressly declare what they intended, they sent back a letter to the king of equal size to his own, but wrote nothing more in it than the two large letters **OY** <note>original: "OY" — the Greek word for "no" or "not".</note>, that is, **No**. By this, they intended to show that they wished to answer his great babbling with the single word **No** or **Not**, and that they considered his great threats worth hardly two letters. For one should not always look at how much is spoken, but rather at what is said; since **goodness**, when it is concisely expressed, is to be preferred over long-windedness.

Therefore, even this dog-like, growling letter **R** <note>In the early modern period, the letter "R" was known as the "dog's letter" (*littera canina*) because its trilled sound was thought to resemble a dog's snarl.</note> and the half-moon **C** are not to be despised. # Chapter XVII

### The sixth and last law: namely, that the Fraternity of the R. C. shall remain hidden for a hundred years.

It is the custom of the common rabble,
as soon as they see that something is kept secret and
hidden, to immediately attack it with their
premature judgment in a hostile manner,
just as if such a work flees the light. All evil deeds,
they say, usually happen in darkness
and secrecy; therefore, this work too
dare not present itself openly in the light of day,
or let itself be seen and submitted
to judgment. From this, its evil nature [is said to] appear.
The rabble also persists in this premature
judgment and calls anything that is hidden
a great vice, which is rightly to be fled and
avoided. Exactly such a judgment
was passed in former times upon the **sacred Eleusinian mysteries** <note>original: "sacris Eleusiniis" — the secret religious rites of ancient Greece dedicated to Demeter and Persephone, famous for their vow of silence.</note> and

# Concerning the Laws

-derstanding people <note>The text continues from the previous page: "un-derstanding people." Here, the author sarcastically refers to the common rabble who believe themselves to be "judicious."</note> broke out into such speeches: "Why are such offerings <note>original: "opffer" — referring to the secret rites or sacrifices of the mysteries.</note> permitted—since they are not in themselves dishonorable or shameful—if they are not performed in the open light of day? Or, if they are ashamed of such things before honest people, why are they not entirely rooted out?" This is just as if all honest, hidden things must necessarily be revealed, or as if everything dishonest must be hidden.

Should one, therefore, reveal everything that is hidden in the secret treasures of Princes, or within the chancelleries <note>original: "Cantzleyen" — the high administrative offices where state secrets and legal records were kept.</note>—presenting it to the common people as if it were not acquired in an honorable manner or by right? No understanding person—unless he perhaps possesses a plundering and thievish spirit—would be able to approve of this or judge it to be right.

But as concerns the aforementioned concluding argument <note>original: "Schlußrede" — a technical term for a logical conclusion or syllogism.</note>: if the first premise <note>original: "Proposition"</note> is to be understood as negative <note>original: "negative"</note> (according to the evidence of the terms <note>original: "terminorum"</note> in the minor premise <note>original: "minore"</note>), then both are negative <note>original: "negantes"</note>, and thus can conclude <note>original: "concludiren"</note> nothing <note>In formal logic, a valid conclusion cannot be drawn from two negative premises.</note>. If, however, the other [premise] is set as affirmative <note>original: "affirmatiue"</note>, as is the major premise <note>original: "maior"</note>, then both are affirmative <note>original: "affirmantes"</note> in the second figure <note>original: "secunda figura" — referring to a specific arrangement of a syllogism in Aristotelian logic.</note>, from which nothing follows either. places [there are] many fellow-brothers and drinking-companions; for which reason there is even less [reason] to desist, but rather [this behavior is] presented to others as a commendable example for imitation. The ancient Lacedemonians <note>The Spartans of ancient Greece.</note> used to present their serf-servants <term>serf-servants</term> <note>original: "leibeigne Knechte"—referring to the Helots, whom the Spartans would intentionally force to drink until drunk to show their children how undignified such behavior was.</note> to their children [while they were] so full and foolish, as a mirror and a [source of] horror regarding drunkenness, just as Lycurgus <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta, credited with establishing the city-state's rigorous social and military systems.</note> ordained in their laws. <meta>catchword: The</meta>

Fraternity through the *Fama*, *Confessio*, and other writings to be revealed. <note>The *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614) and *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615) were the two foundational manifestos that first announced the existence of the Rosicrucian Order to the public.</note> And indeed, if we examine the mind of the first founder a little more closely, he intended for this Society to remain hidden for a hundred years; after the passage of which time, it was permitted for them to reveal themselves through writings. However, two hundred years have already passed since the time that such a law was ordained—as was also mentioned above—namely, from the year 1413 until approximately 1613, the time around which the *Fama* was spread abroad.

Although the certain and explicit reasons for such secrecy cannot be known, and can only be guessed at to some degree, it appears nevertheless that this was determined by the opening of the first founder’s tomb, which is said to have occurred around the year 1604. This is calculated from the time of his birth and age, along with the addition of his prophecy: "*after 120 years, I shall be opened*" <note>original: "*post annos 120. patebo*"</note>. For 106 <note>Referring to the 106-year lifespan of the founder, Christian Rosenkreuz.</note> added to 1378 <note>The legendary birth year of the founder.</note> makes 1484; adding 120 to that makes 1604. There have also been some found within this Society who have certainly confirmed this revelation as prophesied by their ancestors.

# On the Laws

## Chapter XVIII

### What damage or benefit might follow for the common good and the arts, whether this Fraternity remained hidden at all times, or had revealed itself.

Until now, we have been unable to notice anything evil or suspicious in the laws ordained for the Fraternity <note>This "Fraternity" refers to the Rosicrucians, a secretive group of scholars and mystics who claimed to possess hidden knowledge of nature and medicine.</note>—unless we, perhaps like the spider, extract poison from fragrant flowers (from which the bees suck the lovely honey). This is not because the poison is actually joined with the honey; rather, the spider transforms the precious honey into poison, in that she converts that which serves her nourishment into her own nature and venom. On the contrary, this does not happen to the bee. <term>Phalangium</term> <note>original: "Phalangium"; a term used in ancient natural history, notably by Pliny the Elder, to describe a specific genus of venomous spider or harvestman.</note> is the name of a small creature, somewhat larger than an ant, with a red head and a black body,

One must separate the evil that has happened
to come along by chance;
distinguish the right use from the abuse,
and enjoy the former. Thus we do not doubt

were established with good zeal and good intentions,
and also carry great weight and influence,

# On the Laws

[They have] no reason to concern themselves with the gold mines of the Indians and pearl-rich rivers, or how they fight against their enemies with elephants, or to inquire after how they conduct their other affairs; so likewise they have nothing to do with our arts and trades. Had the Fraternity <note>referring to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood, a secret society whose manifestos caused a sensation in early 17th-century Europe</note> remained hidden and not revealed itself in its writings, there would nonetheless have been no lack of arts or learned people to be felt, since from their revelation (as they remain unrecognized), the general public <note>original: "Hauffen," referring to the commonality of scholars or the masses</note> has not been improved. Nor has our Germany <note>original: "Teutschland"</note> lacked silver or gold until now, nor in the art of medicine <term>Arzney-Kunst|The early modern practice of pharmacology and healing</term>, or the preservation of health. ### of the **Fraternity** R. C. <note>original: "Fraternitet R. C."; referring to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood</note>

For, they say, by remaining secret, people would not have been given any guidance toward vain and useless worry, with which they (though in vain) attempt to attain [the Fraternity's secrets]; nor would many have forgotten their own business to pursue those who are still hidden. Many great **calumnies** <term>Calumniẽ|Slanderous or malicious false statements intended to damage a reputation</term> and curses against the Brothers—who show themselves nowhere—would have been avoided; the Echo <note>Likely a reference to the "Echo" of the fraternity's call, or specific contemporary pamphlets titled *Echo* that responded to the Rosicrucian manifestos, often waiting in vain for a physical meeting with the secret order</note> would have waited in vain for their answer; and the hope and desire of many, which they drew from the Fraternity's promises, would not have found themselves utterly deceived and filled with distress. 

In sum, no cause would have been given for many great disorders. But we will not treat further of these claims, as they are all public falsehoods, seeing as the necessity of the matter—one way or the other—can easily be concluded from what was set down above. We proceed now to the benefit that has arisen from the opening <note>meaning the public revelation or publication of their manifestos</note> of this **Society** <note>original: "Societet"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the critical examination of the Rosicrucian Brotherhood. # Of the Laws

And indeed, favorable reader, there is now in this world such a rich abundance of all things—such a distinction and multitude of *species* <note>original: "Specierum"; referring to the distinct types or categories of things in nature</note> and *individuals* <note>original: "Individuorum"; referring to specific, singular examples of those types</note> of all singular and common matters—that one cannot easily perceive the increase or decrease of any one in particular, let alone of the entire *universe* <note>original: "Vniuerſi"</note> as a whole. For this reason, even those persons who, during their lifetimes, were held in the highest regard and seemed very useful and necessary to the world, bring no *deficiency* <note>original: "Defect"; a lack or failure in the cosmic order</note> or lack to the universe or the common world when they depart in death, since others are always accustomed to follow in their place. And there is almost nothing pertaining to human nourishment <note>In this context, "Nahrung" refers generally to what sustains human life or society</note> that a person could not do without, if they had something else to use in its stead. It is likewise the case here: if this society had remained entirely hidden, the world would nonetheless have been whole and perfect in itself. For an unknown good is not easily desired, nor is its absence recognized or its benefit perceived.

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the world is an inherently complete and abundant system where no single individual or group is indispensable. Consequently, he suggests that the Rosicrucian Brotherhood's decision to reveal itself was unnecessary, as the world would have remained perfect even if the society had stayed secret.</summary>

### of the Fraternity of the R. C.

<meta>catchword: not</meta>

And there is no doubt that many such things are hidden in nature, just as no one could have imagined that there was yet a new world—now called <term>America</term>—before <term>Christopher Columbus</term> discovered and revealed it. No one could have conceived of the splendor and utility of the noble art of <term>Printing</term> <note>original: "Truckerey"; the German term for the then-revolutionary technology of the printing press</note> before it was brought into use; before this, a great multitude of scribes sought their livelihood solely by copying the works of authors. Yet, even without such inventions, the world would have suffered no lack, nor did anyone perceive the absence of such unknown and magnificent works before they were made manifest.

Therefore, we perceive in practice that we have received significant benefits from both of these now-revealed inventions, as the previous <term>perfection</term> <note>original: "Vollkommenheit"; implying a state of being "finished" or "complete"</note> of the world has been excellently increased through them.

# Of the Fraternity of the R.C.

...sophical Matter / or <term>Subjectum</term> <note>original: "Subjectum" — the base material or "First Matter" upon which the alchemist performs their work.</note>, in which there are opposing forces. This is now not easily recognized / because it is everywhere hidden / and at the same time also manifest / hidden according to the intellect / but [revealed] according to the words / and even when it is recognized / its preparation remains hidden nonetheless. Its mother is the Philosophical Earth / whose power and property / because they are unknown / [allow] this giant *Antæus* to be secretly strengthened by his mother / and thus every effort applied to kill him is used in vain. <note>In this allegory, the "matter" cannot be transformed (killed/dissolved) until the philosopher understands the "Earth" or the specific matrix it comes from.</note> *Osiris* also, when he traveled to *India* / (as the *Egyptians* report) assigned to *Isis* [the figure of] *Prometheus* as a counselor / *Mercury* as a regent / and *Hercules* as the governor over the *provinces* / whose counsel / art / and labor she should use in her affairs / that is / in *Vulcan’s* workshop / and the golden temple / in which *Apis* was honored. <note>These mythological figures represent alchemical principles: Vulcan's workshop is the furnace; Prometheus is foresight; Mercury is the transformative spirit; and Hercules is the physical labor of the "Great Work." Apis, the sacred bull, often represents the earth or the fixed physical state.</note> For the gifts and help of these three arts are necessarily required in the execution of the Philosophical Work.

When some allow their thoughts to strike more vehemently toward a change of **polities** <note>original: "Policeyen"; referring to the civil administration, social order, and governance of a state.</note>, **religion**, or the free arts, they pursue even the slightest opportunities to put such a thing into effect. This now is the cause of many changes in governments, which commonly take their beginning from false delusions and self-opinion, as can be clearly seen in the **Anabaptists** <note>original: "Widertäuffern"; a radical wing of the Reformation often used as a shorthand for religious and social anarchy in this period.</note> and **enthusiasts** <note>original: "Schwärmern"; literally 'swarmers,' a term used for religious fanatics or radicals who claimed direct divine inspiration.</note>. And it is also beyond all doubt that regarding this, many are found in these our times who, out of a lust for innovation, desire more the disruption of many things, and deliberate among themselves, and [imagine] for themselves a special ***Platonic*** <note>original: "Platonische"; likely referring to the concept of the "Platonic Year" or a radical, idealist restructuring of the world as described in Plato's Republic.</note> change of the arts—

<meta>catchword: sten/</meta>

<summary>This page argues that social and religious upheavals are often the result of individuals projecting their internal desires and delusions onto the world, much like dreams. It warns that many people in the current age seek "innovation" and "disruption" under the guise of religious or philosophical reform.</summary>

<keywords>desire, psychology of dreams, social order, religious radicalism, Anabaptists, enthusiasts, innovation, Platonic change, political instability</keywords>

### Of the Laws

[politi]cs and **religion**. When these people receive an uncertain report of the **fame** and **confession** <note>These terms refer specifically to the *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614) and the *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615), the two foundational manifestos that announced the existence of the Rosicrucian Order to Europe.</note> of this **society**, they immediately fall into the delusion that they now have the opportunity to satisfy their desires.

Because these Brothers are so well provided with magnificent wealth and power, as well as wisdom and understanding, [people believe] they would want to and could begin and carry out a **universal reformation** <note>original: "allgemeine Reformation"; a central 17th-century Rosicrucian concept involving a total spiritual, social, and scientific renewal of the world.</note> in the world. Therefore, they spread their intentions without care, and under the authority of this **society**, they cloak, confirm, and defend them: namely, that the time is now at hand for a single kingdom, one **religion**, and unity among all the divisions practiced until now; and that the **Jews** shall be converted; and just as we **Gentiles** <note>original: "Heyden"; here referring to the non-Jewish Christian world.</note> received our **Messiah**, the Lord **Jesus Christ**, from them, the Jews, they shall likewise receive theirs from us—and whatever other such dreams there may be.

That such things are nothing but worthless, mere imagination, dreams, and fiction, we wish to explain in this chapter. For
